If James was that central, if Jerusalem held him in that kind of regard, if even Paul named him first among the pillars, then why did the tradition find him so difficult to carry cleanly into the centuries that followed? The answer lives in a single Greek word. A word that Jerome in the 4th century decided needed to mean something other than what it said. Jerome is one of the most learned men the early church produced. This is not flattery offered to soften what comes next. It is a fact that makes what happened with James more interesting and more worth thinking about carefully because what Jerome did was not the work of an ignorant person or a careless one. It was the work of someone who understood exactly what was at stake and made a deliberate and considered choice about how to handle it. Understanding that choice and understanding why it was made tells us more about what happened to James's memory than almost anything else in this story. In the late 4th century, Jerome undertook the translation of the Bible into Latin. The result known eventually as the Vulgate became the authoritative scriptural text of the western church for more than a thousand years. When ordinary Christians in medieval Europe encountered the words of scripture, they encountered them in Jerome's Latin. When theologians argued about what a passage meant, they argued about Jerome's Latin. When a priest read from the Bible at a mass, the congregation heard Jerome's Latin. The translation was not simply a text. It was the lens through which an entire civilization read the story it had organized itself around. The weight of that is difficult to overstate. And Jerome had a problem. The problem was a word. The Greek word adelfos. brother. The word Paul had used without explanation in his letter to the Galatians when he wrote that he had seen James, the Lord's brother. The word the Gospels used when they referred to the brothers of Jesus appearing at the edge of his ministry, asking for him, not believing him, urging him toward Jerusalem. In standard Greek, a delos means a full biological sibling, a person who shares both parents with you. That is the plain and primary meaning of the word. The meaning any Greek reader [music] in the first century would have understood without needing to be told. But by Jerome's time, the belief that Mary had remained a virgin, not only at the moment of Jesus's birth, but throughout her entire life, had become theologically significant to a substantial portion of the church. This was not a peripheral concern. The perpetual virginity of Mary was becoming part of how the western church understood the nature of Jesus, the nature of holiness, and the nature of what it meant for God to enter the world in the specific way Christians believed God had entered it. And if Mary was perpetually virgin, she could not have had other children after Jesus, which meant that the people the texts called the brothers of Jesus could not be what the word plainly appeared to say they were. Jerome argued his way to a solution. He pointed out, and he was not wrong about this, that in the tradition of Hebrew and Aramaic usage, words for kinship were often used more broadly than their Greek equivalents in the Hebrew scriptures. The word that gets translated as brother is sometimes used for relationships we would call cousin or kinsman or simply close relative. Jerome's conclusion was that the brothers of Jesus in the gospel texts were in fact cousins, relatives of some kind, kin in the wider seemetic sense of the word rather than siblings in the full biological sense. The linguistic argument is not invented from nothing. It has real substance. Scholars continue to debate its implications for how the gospel texts should be read. and honest scholarship has to acknowledge that the question is genuinely complex. But Jerome was not the only tradition that had wrestled with this problem and it matters to say so clearly in the Eastern church a different solution had already been developed. older than Jerome's and with its own internal logic. This tradition associated with a writer named Epiphanius of Salamus held that the brothers of Jesus were the children of Joseph from a marriage before he wed Mary. Joseph was a widowerower. He had children from that earlier life. When he married Mary, those children became part of the household. And they were the people the gospels called the brothers and sisters of Jesus. Not Mary's children, Joseph's, step-bros, and stepsisters in the household. Half siblings in the extended sense, but not children born to Mary after Jesus. two traditions, two different solutions to the same underlying problem. The Eastern Church saw the brothers as stepchildren of Joseph. The Western church, following Jerome, saw them as cousins. Neither tradition required what the plain Greek word said they were. What neither tradition paused to fully ask was the question underneath the question, not what the brothers of Jesus needed to be for the doctrine of Mary's perpetual virginity to hold, but what it would mean for James specifically, if the word meant what it plainly said, what it would mean to be a full brother of the man who became the center of a world religion, to have grown up in the same house, at the same table through the same ordinary years of childhood and early life before any of it had the shape it later acquired. To have known Jesus before anyone called him Christ to have argued with him. Perhaps the way brothers argue to have watched him become something that neither of you could have predicted and that neither of you had a map for. A full brother of Jesus carries a kind of intimacy with the story that no other category allows. A cousin can be a follower, a believer, someone who encountered the movement and recognized something true in it. A cousin's relationship to Jesus is mediated by belief, by decision, by the choice to follow. A full brother's relationship is prior to all of that. It is simply there, built into the structure of a shared life before any of the questions that would define the movement had even been asked. That kind of intimacy is harder to manage in a tradition that has organized itself around specific understandings of who Jesus was and how he came into the world. A full brother asks questions that a cousin does not. Questions about the household in Nazareth. About what it was like to be in that family. about what Mary was and what Joseph was and what ordinary life looked like before. The story became the story that reshaped the world. Those questions are not comfortable. They are not easily assimilated into the theological frameworks that had developed by Jerome's time. And so the word that contained them was translated into something the story could carry more smoothly, not erased. translated into something the tradition could hold without the full weight of what a brother actually means pressing against the seams. Jerome finished his translation. The Vulgate went into the world for more than a thousand years. The Western church read the word that Paul had written, the Lord's brother. through the lens of Jerome's decision. The cousin's reading became so thoroughly embedded in Western Christian understanding that for most of that period, it was not experienced as an interpretation at all. It was simply what the text said. James remained in the texts. He remained in the letter that carries his name. In the references that dot the book of Acts, in the brief but significant appearances in Paul's letters, he was never removed. The tradition did not try to pretend he had not existed. But the word that had once placed him closest to the center of the story, the word that connected him to Jesus in the most immediate and irreducible way possible, had been quietly moved into a different register, and when a word moves, something moves with it. Not the fact of James, something subtler and harder to name, the weight of his proximity, the specific gravity of what it means to have been that close to where everything began. And then 8 years after James died, the city where he had spent his life was gone. The temple burned in the year 70. The community that had gathered around James, the Jewish followers of Jesus centered in Jerusalem, scattered, the center that James had spent 30 years holding, did not survive his death by long. The Christianity that grew and spread and shaped the centuries that followed was built from the pieces that had already been carried outward into the Greekeaking cities along the Roman roads into the gentile communities Paul had planted far from the place where everything had started. That Christianity remembered Peter. It remembered Paul James. it remembered in the form that the story found it easiest to carry. A cousin rather than a brother, a writer of a useful letter rather than the pillar named before Peter, a figure at the edge of the tradition rather than the man who held its center for a generation. The word had changed, and the man in some important way had changed with it. Go back to Jerusalem. Go back to the year 62 to the paragraph in Josephus that opened this story. You have read it before. You know what it says. Ananas convenes a council. James is brought before it. He is condemned. He is killed. The most fair-minded people in the city protest. The incoming Roman governor is angered. Ananas loses his position. Raid it again now. The words are the same. The sentences are the same. The spare non- theological language of a historian recording events for an audience that has no particular investment in any of the people involved has not changed by a single syllable. Josephus is still doing what Josephus does, which is observe and record and move on without pausing to assign meaning or draw lessons or tell the reader how to feel. But you are not the same reader you were at the beginning. You know now that the man Anus moved against was not a minor community leader in a city full of competing religious factions. You know that Paul named him first among the pillars of the movement before Peter before John. In a letter Paul wrote to defend his own authority. You know that James presided over the most consequential decision the earliest Christian community ever made and that the decision was effectively closed when James spoke. You know that Jerusalem called him the just, called him the bullwok, gave him titles that cities do not give to people whose absence would not be felt. You know that his knees told the story of what he had given to the prayers that defined his daily life. When Ananis moves against James, you understand now what is actually being moved against. Not a man, a center. The historians who have looked most carefully at this moment tend to read Aninus's motivation as political. The gap between governors was an opportunity. And Aninus was someone who recognized opportunities. James led a community that had been growing in influence and visibility in Jerusalem for three decades. The charges of violating the law were the available legal mechanism for doing what Aninus wanted to do. which was to act while he could. That reading is probably right as far as it goes. But there is something else in the timing that I find myself returning to. Something that the political reading does not fully account for. James had been in Jerusalem for 30 years. He had been the recognized leader of his community for most of that time. He had negotiated the complicated terrain of a city under Roman occupation. A city with its own internal religious politics, a city full of factions and pressures and competing claims on what it meant to be faithful. He had done all of this while maintaining the respect according to both Josephus and Hegypus of people who did not share his beliefs. That is not an easy thing to sustain for 30 years. That is the work of someone who understood the world he was living in and had figured out how to remain inside it without losing what he was. And then Ananas decides that now is the moment 8 years before Jerusalem falls. I am not suggesting a causal connection in any literal sense. History is not arranged that neatly. And the traditions that did suggest such a connection were doing theology, not history. But I am saying that the timing sits there in the record and does not let you move past it without feeling something. James died holding the center of a world that was 8 years away from ceasing to exist. The community he led, the Jewish Christianity of Jerusalem, did not survive the destruction of the temple as a coherent and continuing force. It scattered. Some went across the Jordan, others dissolved into the wider Jewish diaspora. The particular form of faith James had spent his life sustaining. rooted in Jerusalem, rooted in the temple, rooted in the daily practice of a Jewish life, lived in the place where Jewish life had its deepest roots. That form of faith did not make it through the year 70 in any form that could carry forward independently. What made it through was what Paul had built. And what Paul had built was located somewhere else entirely. The communities Paul planted were in Corinth and Ephesus and Philippi and Rome. They were Greekeaking. They were majority gentile. They did not depend on Jerusalem for their existence or their legitimacy in any practical sense. Because Paul had spent his ministry arguing very successfully that they did not need to. They had their own letters from Paul. They had their own leadership. They had their own way of understanding what the movement was and what it was for. A way that did not require the temple to be standing or Jerusalem to be intact. When Jerusalem fell, those communities lost a symbolic center. They did not lose a functional one. They kept going. They grew. They produced the texts and the arguments and the institutional structures that became what we recognize as Christianity. And in their memory, in the story they told about where they had come from and what the movement was. James occupied a complicated position. He was in the texts. He could not be removed from the texts because Paul had written about him and the book of Acts had recorded him and those documents were already circulating and already considered authoritative. But he was in the texts as a problem that had to be managed rather than a center that had to be honored. He was the pillar named first by Paul who ended up third or fourth in popular memory. He was the leader of Jerusalem who became a footnote to the people who had left Jerusalem. He was the Lord's brother who became a cousin when a cousin fit better into the story the tradition needed to tell about Mary and about Jesus and about how God had entered the world. He was not forgotten. He was remembered in the shape the surviving tradition found it most convenient to give him. Now I want to leave you with three questions. Not because I have been saving the answers for a dramatic reveal at the end. I have no answers to give you. These are questions that do not have answers that any amount of scholarship or any length of reflection has managed to close. They are the kind of questions that stay open, that work on you after you have stopped actively thinking about them, that come back in unexpected moments and ask to be sat with again. The first question. If James was the settled authority in Jerusalem, if Paul named him first among the pillars, if the book of Acts records the most consequential early decision of the movement being effectively closed when James spoke, what does that ask us to reconsider about the story many people inherited? The story where Peter and Paul stand in the foreground and James stands somewhere behind them, mentioned occasionally, explained carefully, remembered as secondary to the people who traveled farther and wrote more and whose names ended up on more churches. This is not a question designed to reduce Peter or Paul. What they did was real and its consequences were enormous. But when a story moves from one world into another, something gets carried and something gets left at the border. The question is whether we are willing to look at what was left, not to judge the people who made the crossing, but to understand what the crossing actually cost. The second question, James survives most clearly in the sources that were not trying to build what Christianity became. He survives in Josephus, who was a Jewish historian writing for Romans and had no investment whatsoever in making James into a saint or a symbol or a useful figure for any theological argument. He survives in Hegosipus who was remembering a Jerusalem that had ceased to exist by the time he was writing, preserving a portrait that served no obvious institutional purpose and that looks nothing like the leaders the surviving church was producing in his own time. He survives at the margins in the places where the tradition's need to shape its own memory was weakest and the older material had the most room to breathe. What does it mean that our clearest picture of James comes from people who were not trying to serve the story that won? That question does not resolve into a simple answer about conspiracy or institutional failure or deliberate suppression. It resolves into something quieter and more unsettling. It resolves into a question about how memory works when a movement grows beyond the world that produced it. About what happens to the people who stayed in that world when the people who left begin to write the history. About whether a tradition can look honestly at the gap between the people it chose to remember and the people who were actually there. without that honesty requiring it to tear down everything it built on the way out. The third question, this is the one I find hardest. Jerusalem fell in the year 70. The temple burned. The world James had given his life to the world of Jewish practice and temple prayer and the specific geography of a city that understood itself as the place where the sacred and the ordinary met every day. That world did not come back. What replaced it as the center of Christian life and memory and institutional authority was Rome. And what replaced the Jewish movement James had led was something genuinely and powerfully new. Something that had translated itself so completely into a different world that its origins in the first one had become difficult to see clearly. What would Christianity have become if Jerusalem had not fallen? if the community James led had survived, not as a footnote absorbed into a gentile church that had already moved past it, but as a continuing and living center, if the movement had maintained a heart that was still Jewish, still temple oriented, still shaped by the rhythms of practice and the geography of the city where it began. We cannot know the history went the way it went. And counterfactual history is always closer to imagination than to scholarship. I know that. But I keep asking it anyway. Not because I think the outcome would necessarily have been better. Not because I am romanticizing a lost world or suggesting that what Paul built was a mistake, but because the question does something that I think is worth doing. It restores weight to what was actually lost. It refuses to treat the fall of Jerusalem and the disappearance of Jewish Christianity as simply the natural and inevitable tide of history. As if what survived was always destined to survive and what was lost was never really loadbearing. James was loadbearing to the people who called him the bull work to the city that gave him that name. to the tradition that kept fragments of his memory alive in texts that were never quite sure what to do with him. And are we allowed to grieve the Christianity that might have been? I think we are. I think that grief is one of the ways we honor the people that history found inconvenient to remember fully. James, the Lord's brother, the man who stayed, the pillar named first and inherited last, deserves at minimum that much from us. He stood in Jerusalem for 30 years. He prayed on stone floors until his knees became the record of it. He held something together that nothing else could hold once he was gone. 8 years after they killed him, the city he had given his life to was burning. Whether the connection between those two facts is history or something that lives in the space between history and meaning, I leave with you. The texts are there. The questions are open. James is waiting to be heard.

Topic 4:
This was happening in the world at the time Jesus was born. While Jesus was being born in a cave in Judea, Rome controlled Israel and the entire Mediterranean. But Rome was no longer a republic. It was ruled by the first Roman emperor, Caesar Augustus. And believe it or not, you know him well. Before proclaiming himself emperor, he was Octavian, the general adopted by Julius Caesar, who finally brought down Mark Anthony and the famed Cleopatra, the last queen of Egypt. Augustus was the most powerful man on the planet. He had established the Pax Romana, peace enforced by the sword. His order reached from the shores of Hispania to the deserts of Syria and from Gaul all the way to Egypt. But Rome had a mighty enemy in the east. Persia, the Paththean Empire, a vast kingdom that stretched from Mesopotamia almost to India. The ancient lands we now know as Iran, Iraq, and Turkey. They were the heirs of ancient Persia. Ruled by Freightes IV, a brutal king who seized the throne by murdering his own father and 30 brothers. Persia was the only superpower that dared stand up to Rome. And after the war, Emperor Augustus offered a peace treaty and sent a gift, an Italian slave named Musa. But Musa was no ordinary slave. She was stunningly beautiful, intelligent, and ambitious. She quickly captivated the brutal king, becoming his favorite wife, and bearing him a son, Freightes V. Now, Musa had a son, an heir. But there was a problem. The king's other sons had stronger claims to the throne. So the cunning queen convinced her husband to send his four eldest sons to Rome for a proper education. In reality, she was clearing her path to power. And once her rivals were far away, only one obstacle remained, the king himself. One night, when he was no longer of use to her, Musa served him a cup of poisoned wine at his own table. The brutal King Freightes IV dropped dead. And just like that, a Roman slave took her seat on the mighty throne of the Persian Empire, ruling alongside her son. This was the kind of political madness and betrayal shaking the world. But Moose's story doesn't end here. First, we need to stop and look at something important that happened during this time. Persia was a land of magi, astrologers, and ancient religions. This is where the so-called wise men came from. They weren't actually kings, and they didn't arrive the night Jesus was born. They were magi, high-ranking astronomer priests from Persia, men of science and faith who saw something extraordinary in the sky, most likely a rare conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn, a sign that, according to their calculations, announced the birth of a unique king in Judea. And they followed that sign in the sky for 2 years until they reached Jerusalem. The three Paththean dignitaries enjoyed diplomatic immunity and requested an audience with Herod the Great, the man Rome had installed to rule Judea to ask where the king of the Jews had been born. Herod was not born a king. Rome had appointed him. He had no blood of David. And these foreigners spoke of someone who was born to reign. Nervous, Herod summoned the chief priests, and they gave him the answer. According to prophecy, the Messiah would be born in Bethlehem of Judea and would be from the lineage of David. The people of Israel were still waiting for the Messiah, the Savior promised by many prophets who would come to free them from slavery and bring them justice and peace. But they were in the longest prophetic silence in history. More than 400 years without a prophet, without a sign, and their faith had grown corrupt. That's why even though Bethlehem was only a couple of hours away on foot, none of the priests went to verify if the Savior had really been born. Only the foreigners, the wise men from Persia, followed the star in the sky to Bethlehem. There they found Jesus, no longer a baby, but a one-year-old child. They knelt and offered him three prophetic gifts: gold, frankincense, and myrr for his death. And this will be important because this gold was what funded Mary, Joseph, and the child's escape to Egypt when King Herod ordered the killing of all boys under two years old in Bethlehem and the surrounding areas. They arrived in just a few weeks thanks to one of the greatest inventions of that era, the Roman road system. Under Caesar Augustus' command, Rome wo together more than 50,000 m of paved roads. That's like circling the entire earth five times on stone highways. And the starting point was Rome. This is where the phrase all roads lead to Rome was born. Remember this. It will be important later because the very same roads used to control the world would be the ones the first Christians traveled to carry the name of Jesus to the farthest corners of the empire. But it wasn't the only great construction project of that time. In China, work continued on the Great Wall. Mile after mile of stone and earth stacked by hand. A massive wall stretching like a serpent across the mountains to hold back enemies from the north. We're in the year 1 BC and the Great Wall already measured over 3,700 m. That wall protected the other great empire of the world, Han Dynasty, China, a superpower with nearly 60 million people as vast and powerful as Rome itself. And on the throne of all that power sat a child, Emperor Ping. He was only eight or nine years old when he was crowned son of heaven, the absolute sovereign of China. Yet, he was nothing more than a puppet. Emperor Ping was a prisoner in a golden cage. He couldn't choose what to eat for breakfast. He couldn't leave the palace. He couldn't have friends. Behind the throne stood the true ruler, a man named Wang Mang. He was a relative of the imperial family, appointed regent to care for the young emperor. Wangm was brilliant, calculating, and ruthlessly ambitious. But he didn't seem evil. Quite the opposite, he was a public relations genius. He marketed himself as a humble scholar of the classics. He publicly donated his salary and lived with austerity, winning the hearts of the people. But it was all a mask. While he smiled in public, he placed his allies in key positions. He eliminated his rivals through quiet exiles or by forcing them to take their own lives. And his final move was to marry his own daughter to the child emperor, tightening his grip even further. You might think that back then there were no connections. That what happened in the Far East remained unknown in the West, but that's not true. When Herod died, Jesus returned to Judea while still a child and grew up in Nazareth. Nazareth was a village of barely 300 people, but it held a secret. Just 6 km from Nazareth, an hour's walk away, ran one of the ancient world's most important highways, the Via Maris. The Viamaris, meaning the way of the sea, was a major trade route connecting the great empires from Egypt to Mesopotamia and beyond, linking up with the famous Silk Road that stretched all the way to China. The Silk Road was a massive web spanning 8,000 km, connecting China to Rome through Central Asia, Persia, India, and the Arab world. Its name came from one of its star products, Chinese silk. so valuable that the Roman Senate tried to ban it because it was draining the empire's gold reserves. Picture caravans of hundreds of camels trudging for months through endless deserts loaded with silk, spices, incense, precious stones, Roman glass, ivory, and manuscripts. The Silk Road's vast network of paths covered more distance than any Roman highway. A complete journey from one end to the other could take months, sometimes more than a year, but very few people made the entire trip. Goods typically changed hands many times along the way. Caravans passed through carrying Chinese silk, Indian spices, Arabian incense, and Roman glass. But the most important thing traveling along that route was invisible. Ideas traveled the world. Buddhism made its way from India to China along this route. Babylonian astronomy, mathematics, and news flowed through it. For the first time in history, a message could originate at one end of the world and reach the other side in just weeks. The Via Maris was one of the main arteries connecting the heart of Rome to the far east through the Silk Road. So even though Nazareth was tucked away in the hills, just a few miles away ran the great highway where armies, caravans, and news passed by, linking the west to the far east. Jesus didn't grow up cut off from the world. He grew up watching global trade, imperial armies, and eastern ideas pass by. He grew up watching the world go by, hidden in plain sight. God chose to hide his son where no one would look for him, yet where everyone would see him without recognizing him. And from the hills of Nazareth, a young Jesus could see the Valley of Armageddon, the stage for the final battle. And as if that weren't enough, just an hour's walk away stood a dazzling city, Sepharus. Herod Antipass was rebuilding it to become the jewel of Galilee. A cosmopolitan capital with a theater, mosaics, bustling markets, and Romanstyle villas. It's very likely that Joseph and a young Jesus worked there as carpenters, rubbing shoulders with people from every nation, hearing languages from all over the world. And while the child Jesus was growing up, on the other side of the world, another child was starting to ask questions. In the year 6 AD, Emperor Ping was already 14 years old. He was beginning to want to rule for real, and that didn't suit Wang Mang at all. One day, during a celebration, Wang Mang offered the young emperor a cup of medicinal wine to wish him a long life. Days later, the boy was dead. The official story was that he died from illness, but historians suspect he was poisoned. The child emperor was murdered by the very man who had promised to protect him. Wang Mang dropped the mask, seized the throne, and proclaimed himself emperor, founding his own dynasty, the Shin Dynasty. Though he attempted radical reforms, his dynasty barely lasted 15 years. He died lynched by a mob. And while all this was happening in China, something was about to crumble in the Paththean Empire. Remember Musa, the Roman slave queen who managed to rule Persia alongside her son? For a few years, Musa's plan seemed perfect until tensions flared over control of Armenia, the hot border between Rome and Persia. Queen Musa decided to seed the territory in exchange for Rome, officially recognizing her son Freightes V as the rightful king of the Paththean Empire. It was a dangerous deal. It secured peace with Rome, but ignited the fury of the Paththean nobles. The aristocracy never accepted that Persia's throne was occupied by the son of an Italian slave, nor that their rulers were bowing to Rome. Then in 4 AD, the nobles had enough. They organized a coup, overthrew Musa and her son, and crowned Arodis III. Musa and her son had to flee in haste to Rome, where Augustus welcomed them as useful pieces in his game of power. And so ended the story of the woman who went from slave in Rome to queen in Persia. Meanwhile, Jesus grew up in Nazareth until he turned 30 and that very day began his ministry. After three decades of waiting, working, and silence, Jesus left the town that watched him grow and walked toward the Jordan River, where a man named John the Baptist was baptizing a long line of people. There Jesus was baptized in the lowest place on earth. Yes, the Jordan River, where Jesus was baptized, flows at 430 m below sea level. It's the lowest land point on the planet. It's also a place marked by beginnings and endings. This is the exact spot where Israel, led by Joshua, first crossed into the promised land, the very same place where the prophet Elijah was taken up to heaven in a chariot of fire. Then the Holy Spirit led him into the vast Judeian wilderness. There Jesus fasted for 40 days and 40 nights. And after overcoming Satan's temptation, Jesus returned home. He went back to Nazareth and filled with the power of the spirit, entered the synagogue. But the people who had watched him grow up weren't ready for his message. His own hometown rejected him. He would never return to Nazareth again. But he didn't stop. He traveled to the shores of Galilee and called his first disciples. He performed his first miracle in Canaa. and he chose the bustling town of Capernaum to settle down with his followers. For 3 years, Jesus traveled throughout Judea and Galilee. In Kaa, he healed a Roman centurion servant without even being there. Then he went to Naen and raised a widow's only son from the dead. He visited Corazin, preaching and healing. He raised Lazarus in Bethany. Crowds gathered at the gates, searching for a word for hope. the sick, the oppressed, those who had lost everything. They came to him with hearts heavy with despair. And then the miracles began. Jerusalem, Perea, Jericho, Gdara. At that time, life in the land of Israel revolved around a single belief, Judaism. This was the religion of the Jewish people, and their faith rested on three pillars. First, the Torah, the law that God gave to Moses, describing how to live according to God's will. Second, the prophets, people chosen by God to proclaim his word and guide the nation. And third, the writings, books filled with wisdom, prayers, and songs. But they were also influenced by Roman beliefs. The Romans greatly admired Greek culture. So they simply adopted their pantheon and changed the names. Zeus became Jupiter. Hia became Juno. Athena became Manurva. These were powerful gods. Capricious and deeply human. To the east in mighty Persia, the dominant religion was Zoroastrianism. Their prophet Zarathustra had lived many centuries before Christ. They believed in a good and creator god, Ahura Mazda, who battled against an evil spirit named Angrau. For them, the history of the world was a struggle between light and darkness, between good and evil. They believed in judgment after death, in the purification of the soul, and in a new life beyond this one. This matters because Persia had ruled over the land of Judea before Greece and Rome ever did. Many ideas about resurrection, the hierarchy of angels, and the nature of demons that we see in Jesus' time grew stronger through this blending of cultures. But the world was much larger than that. If you traveled even farther east, you'd reach India. There, ancient traditions like Hinduism and Buddhism were already offering paths of wisdom and liberation to millions of people. These spiritual paths taught how to find wisdom and inner peace. They believed people were born again and again in a cycle called reincarnation. Buddha, the founder of Buddhism, taught that inner peace could be reached by letting go of suffering. And beyond that, in distant China, people sought balance in different ways. One of these paths was Confucianism, which taught how to live respectfully with others. It didn't speak of gods, but of duties and order. There was also Tauism, which taught that everyone should follow the Tao, meaning the way, a path of living in harmony with nature. And many people practiced a folk religion that honored the spirits of their ancestors and local gods. Back in the Mediterranean in ancient Egypt, people still worshiped old gods like Rah, Isis, and Osiris. But by now, their religion had blended with Greek and Roman beliefs. And in the forests of Europe, Celtic and Germanic tribes called upon the forces of nature through their Druids. They believed in invisible powers and spirits of the earth and sky. The world was filled with religions, ideas, and questions. But what was happening at that same moment on the American continent? In the jungles of Central America and the mountains of the Andes, great civilizations were rising, though in silence unknown to the rest of the world. In what is now Mexico, the Maya were experiencing a period of growth that scientists today call the late pre-classic. These weren't scattered tribes. They were building monumental cities like Elm Mirador with massive pyramids that rivaled those of Egypt in sheer volume. They had writing, mathematics, and an obsession with time and the stars. And something remarkable was taking shape near present-day Mexico City. Right around the time of Christ, the city of Teotiwakan was being planned and built. Centuries later, it would be known as the city of the gods, a vast and orderly metropolis. Further south in the deserts of what is now Peru, the Nazca culture had begun creating their famous lines in the earth, giant figures that can only be seen from the sky, and the Mochce were building kingdoms with temples and adobe sculptures. Meanwhile, in the northern part of the continent, in what is now the United States, the Hopewell culture was constructing large burial mounds. They also had trade routes stretching thousands of miles. The world was full of people searching for answers about life, good, evil, and what happens after death. On every side of the globe, in every culture, the questions were similar, but the answers were different. But history was about to be split in two. At that very moment, Jerusalem was preparing for Passover. And Jesus set out for the holy city. He had to arrive in time to deliver a crucial message. The journey was long. He traveled through the villages of Samaria and the region of Perea on the other side of the Jordan River, teaching about the kingdom of God and preparing his followers for the end. But before entering Jerusalem in Bethany, Jesus raised his friend Lazarus from the dead, a man who had been buried for 4 days. It was the most public and defiant miracle of all. There was no turning back now. The news spread like wildfire. All of Jerusalem was talking about the resurrection of Lazarus. This miracle was the last straw for the religious leaders. From that day on, they officially began plotting his death. The end was approaching, and Jesus knew it. It was time to fulfill the purpose for which he had been born, to die. After preaching in Perea and Jericho, he returned to Bethany. There, two weeks before his death, Mary anointed his feet with an incredibly expensive perfume. For my burial, Jesus said quietly. She was the only one who understood. The exact moment had arrived. Passover, the most important festival for every Jew, and the final week of Jesus's life, wasn't an escape. It was a masterpiece of provocation. It began with his entry into Jerusalem. The news spread like wildfire. The man who had raised Lazarus from the dead was at the city gates. A massive crowd went out to meet him. Then Jesus mounted a young donkey and began descending from the Mount of Olives toward the city. The crowd welcomed him like a king, waving palm branches and shouting, "Hosana! Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord, the King of Israel." Tension in Jerusalem was thick enough to cut with a knife. The religious leaders were desperate to stop Jesus. An opportunity knocked on their door one dark night. Judas Iscariat, one of the 12 disciples, handed over his master for 30 pieces of silver, the price of a slave, the betrayal was sealed. After the last supper, Jesus and his disciples went to a garden called Gethsemane, just outside the walls of Jerusalem. There on the well-known Mount of Olives, Jesus was arrested. The soldiers dragged Jesus through the darkness toward the upper city. It was an exclusive neighborhood where the rich and powerful lived. Here, in a luxurious mansion, Annus waited, the former high priest. Jewish trials were forbidden at night by their own law. Trials during Passover were forbidden. Execution on the same day as the verdict was forbidden. That night, the religious leaders broke every single one of their own rules. From the house of Anna, they took him before Caiaphas, the acting high priest. The Jewish priests ruled like monarchs over their people, but they had no authority to execute anyone. They needed Rome's approval. Then, Caiaphas brought Jesus to the palace of Herod the Great, where Ponteus Pilate now ruled. This was already Jesus' fourth trial in less than a day. And his enemies didn't want a quick death. They wanted the most torturous execution the empire had to offer. Crucifixion reserved only for rebellious slaves and enemies of the state. Pilate questioned Jesus but saw no threat to Rome in him. He went out before the crowd and declared him innocent. The people erupted in fury. Pilate couldn't understand. Why were these same people who days earlier had hailed him as king now demanding his death? These same Jews had welcomed Jesus as king just 5 days before. They believed Jesus would defeat Rome. They wanted a warrior, a political liberator. But now they saw him in chains, spit on, beaten. They saw him as a fraud. Their warrior Messiah was nothing but a weak, helpless prisoner. Disappointment turned to rage and they shouted without stopping, "Crucify him!" Pilate handed Jesus over to his soldiers to be flogged, a brutal punishment that might satisfy the crowd. But when the people saw him even weaker, even more human, they demanded his crucifixion. Pilate gave in. He washed his hands and handed him over to be crucified. Jesus was just hours away from his final breath. The soldiers dragged him out of the pritorium. They loaded the horizontal beam of the cross onto his shoulders. And Jesus walked from the pritorium to Golgatha, passing through the heart of Jerusalem's old city. That path of barely 600 m is today one of the most sacred places in the world. The Via Cruis or Via Dolar Roa. But on that day, it was just an ordinary street turned into a corridor of death. Jesus was so weakened by the flogging that his body collapsed along the way. He fell to the ground, the weight of the wood crushing him into the dust. Finally, he reached the final destination, a rocky hill outside the city walls called Golgatha. Then the moment came. At 9:00 in the morning, they nailed him to a cross. And at 3:00 in the afternoon, the sky went dark and Jesus breathed his last words. It is finished. That was his mission. They didn't take his life. He gave it his entire life. Every miracle, every teaching had led to this precise moment. The purpose for which he was born wasn't to live as a king, but to die as a sacrifice. But the cross wasn't the final destination. It was the doorway, the beginning of something extraordinary. Jesus rose again and for 40 days he appeared in different places across the map. In Jerusalem, in Galilee, on the road to Emmas, more than 500 people saw him. In his final appearance, Jesus gathered with his disciples on the Mount of Olives near Jerusalem. There they witnessed with their own eyes as Jesus ascended into heaven until a cloud hid him from sight. In that final moment, Jesus didn't give them physical proof. Instead, he gave them a promise that would become the most powerful proof of all. He promised that God's spirit would come down upon them and that this spirit would draw people to him throughout history. 50 days later, it happened. At Pentecost, the disciples left their fear behind and rushed into the streets, proclaiming that the king had conquered death. 3,000 people were baptized that day. Within just a few years, the message had spread across the entire Roman Empire. The mission was clear. The master had defeated death. And now he was entrusting them to carry this message to the whole world. Death had been conquered. The promise had been made. They were just a handful of fishermen and tax collectors. And the future rested in their hands. But they weren't alone. Uncertainty had given way to conviction. Fear turned into courage. The mission Jesus had given them was clear. And with the Holy Spirit as their guide, they were ready to change the world. No successful movement in human history has ever started this way with its leader publicly executed. Back then there was one golden rule that never failed. Kill the leader and you kill the revolution. The Romans were experts at this. When they killed Judas of Galilee, his followers scattered. When they executed Theudus, his movement vanished into thin air. No leader, no cause. Pure logic. Yet with Jesus of Nazareth, a historical anomaly occurred that no emperor could explain. The 12 disciples embarked on missionary journeys across the entire world. Philip preached in Syria and Asia Minor all the way to what is now Turkey. Matthew in Persia and Ethiopia. Andrew as far as Greece, Thomas all the way to India, Bartholomew in Armenia, and Paul who traveled more than any other apostle. He journeyed through Asia Minor, Greece, Syria, Macedonia, Italy. He founded churches in strategic cities like Ephesus, Thessalonica, Philippi, Corinth, and Rome. But how could 12 ordinary men conquer the world with nothing but their words? The story of the apostles is incredible. If you want to know the journey each of them took until their death, you need to watch this video appearing on screen. You'll discover how from the streets of Jerusalem to the ends of the earth, these 12 men carried a message that would challenge kings, tear down temples, and split history in two. Armed with nothing but faith in what they had witnessed. This is the story of the birth of the church and how these 12 disciples conquered the world with nothing but their faith and what they sacrificed to make it happen. Don't miss it because their story lives on in us today. Blessings to you and your family.

topic 6
Se você quer conhecer a história de Jesus, leia Mateus, Marcos ou Lucas. Mas se você quer conhecer a mente de Deus, leia João. Este livro não começa com anjos, pastores ou um nascimento virginal. Ele ignora completamente a história humana e nos lança diretamente nas profundezas infinitas da eternidade. João quer despedaçar suas expectativas. Ele quer que você compreenda que o homem que caminhou por estradas empoeiradas, chorou com seus amigos e sangrou em uma cruz, é o mesmíssimo Deus que pronunciou as galáxias à existência. Ele o chama de o verbo. No princípio era o verbo e o verbo estava com Deus e o verbo era Deus. Por meio dele, todas as coisas foram feitas. Sem ele, nada do que existe foi feito. Imagine o criador do universo descendo até sua própria criação. Ele não veio como um espírito ou um fantasma, tornou-se um ser humano. Sentiu o vento frio, a fome no estômago e o toque de um amigo. O verbo se fez carne e habitou entre nós. Vimos a sua glória. Glória do Filho único que veio do Pai, cheio de graça e de verdade. Por que ele fez isso? Ele veio para trazer luz a um mundo de trevas. Está escrito que Jesus é a luz que brilha nas trevas e as trevas não a venceram. Antes que o Messias se revelasse, Deus enviou um homem para preparar o caminho. Seu nome era João Batista. Ele não era um rei em um palácio. Era um homem rude que vivia no deserto. Batizava as pessoas no rio Jordão, chamando-as ao arrependimento dos seus pecados. Os líderes religiosos enviaram sacerdotes e levitas para perguntar-lhe quem ele era. Queriam saber se ele era o Messias, mas João Batista conhecia o seu lugar. Confessou abertamente que ele não era o Cristo, não era a luz, era apenas uma testemunha da luz. Citando o profeta Isaías, declarou ser a voz do que clama no deserto: Endireitai o caminho do Senhor Certo dia, João olhou para cima e viu Jesus vindo em sua direção. Naquele momento, ele pronunciou as palavras que revelaram a missão de Jesus: "Eis o cordeiro de Deus que tira o pecado do mundo." Esta foi uma declaração chocante. Por séculos, o povo judeu havia sacrificado cordeiros para cobrir seus pecados. Agora, João estava dizendo que aquele homem era o sacrifício final. Jesus não apenas cobriria o pecado, ele o tiraria completamente. João Batista testemunhou que viu o Espírito descer do céu como pomba e permanecer sobre Jesus. Por isso, ele dava testemunho de que aquele era o filho de Deus. No dia seguinte, dois dos discípulos de João decidiram seguir Jesus. Um deles era André. Ele estava tão convencido que correu para encontrar seu irmão Simão e dar-lhe a notícia. Achamos o Messias. Quando Jesus encontrou Simão, deu-lhe um novo nome. Chamou-o Cefas, que significa Pedro, ou pedra. Jesus já estava construindo o fundamento de sua igreja. No dia seguinte, Jesus encontrou Felipe e disse-lhe: "Segue-me". Felipe então encontrou Natanael. Natanael estava cético. Perguntou se algo de bom poderia vir de uma cidadezinha como Nazaré. Felipe simplesmente respondeu: "Vem e vê". Quando Jesus viu Natanael se aproximando, disse-lhe que o havia visto debaixo da figueira antes mesmo de Felipe chamá-lo. Natanael ficou maravilhado, pois ninguém poderia saber daquilo. Ele declarou: "Rabi, tu és o filho de Deus. Tu és o rei de Israel." Jesus respondeu: "Crês, porque te disse que te vi debaixo da figueira? Coisas maiores do que estas verás." Três dias depois, houve um casamento no povoado de Caná. Jesus, sua mãe Maria e seus discípulos foram convidados. Naqueles dias, um casamento era uma grande celebração que durava vários dias, mas algo deu errado. O vinho acabou. Era um desastre social vergonhoso para a família do noivo. Maria voltou-se para Jesus e disse: "Eles não têm mais vinho. Jesus viu seis talhas de pedra ali perto. Eram usadas para purificações cerimoniais. Cada uma comportava entre 70 e 100 L. Jesus disse aos serventes para encherem as talhas de água e eles as encheram até a borda. Então ele lhes disse para tirarem agora e levarem ao mestre sala. Em algum lugar entre a talha e o copo aconteceu o milagre. Água simples transformou-se em um vinho rico e delicioso. O mestre Sala provou e ficou admirado. Disse ao noivo que todos servem primeiro o melhor vinho. E quando os convidados já estão embriagados, então o pior. Mas ele havia guardado o melhor vinho até aquele momento. Este foi o primeiro sinal que Jesus realizou. manifestou sua glória e seus discípulos creram nele. Mostrou que Jesus traz alegria, abundância e transformação. Jesus então viajou para Jerusalém para a Páscoa. Foi ao pátio do templo, que deveria ser um lugar de oração. Em vez disso, encontrou um mercado. Homens vendiam bois, ovelhas e pombas. Outros estavam sentados às mesas trocando dinheiro. Jesus fez um chicote de cordas. e expulsou-os todos do pátio do templo. Espalhou as moedas dos cambistas e virou suas mesas. Tirai isto daqui. Ele disse: "Não façais da casa de meu pai um mercado." Seus discípulos lembraram-se da Escritura que diz que o zelo pela casa de Deus o consumiria. Os líderes religiosos ficaram irados e pediram um sinal para provar sua autoridade. Jesus respondeu: "Destruí este templo e em três dias o levantarei." Eles pensaram que estava falando do edifício que havia levado 46 anos para ser construído, mas o templo do qual ele falava era o seu corpo. Estava predizendo sua própria morte e ressurreição. Entre os líderes religiosos havia um fariseu chamado Nicodemos, mestre em Israel. Ele via os sinais que Jesus realizava e sabia que Deus devia estar com ele. Veio visitar Jesus de noite. Rabit ele disse: "Sabemos que és um mestre vindo da parte de Deus, pois ninguém pode realizar os sinais que tu fazes se Deus não estiver com ele." Jesus não se concentrou nos elogios, concentrou-se na alma de Nicodemos. Em verdade te digo. Ele respondeu: "Ninguém pode ver o reino de Deus se não nascer de novo." Nicodemos ficou confuso. Perguntou como alguém poderia nascer sendo já velho. Estava pensando no nascimento físico, mas Jesus explicou que falava de um renascimento espiritual. O que nasce da carne é carne, mas o que nasce do espírito é espírito. Jesus lembrou-lhe ainda como Moisés havia levantado a serpente no deserto. O Da mesma forma o Filho do Homem precisava ser levantado para que todo aquele que nele crê tivesse a vida eterna. Naquela conversa silenciosa, Jesus pronunciou as palavras mais famosas de toda a Bíblia. resumiu o evangelho em uma única frase: "Porque Deus amou o mundo de tal maneira que deu o seu filho unigênito, para que todo aquele que nele crê não pereça, mas tenha a vida eterna". Jesus explicou que não veio ao mundo para condená-lo, mas para salvá-lo por meio dele. Jesus deixou a Judeia para voltar à Galileia. Tinha que passar pela Samaria. Os judeus e os samaritanos odiavam-se e recusavam-se a conviver. Cansado da viagem, Jesus sentou-se junto ao poço de Jacó. Era meio-dia, a hora mais quente do dia. Uma mulher samaritana veio buscar água. Vinha sozinha ao meio-dia, provavelmente para evitar o julgamento das outras mulheres da cidade. Jesus quebrou todas as regras sociais ao falar com ela e pedir um pouco de água. A mulher ficou chocada, perguntou como ele, sendo judeu, pedia de beber a ela, que era samaritana. Jesus respondeu: "Se conhecesses o dom de Deus e quem é que te diz: "Dá-me de beber", tu lhe pedirias e ele te daria água viva. Aquele que beber da água que ele daria jamais teria sede, pois aquela água se faria nele uma fonte a jorrar para a vida eterna. A mulher ficou curiosa, queria aquela água para nunca mais ter sede. Mas Jesus gentilmente expôs sua vida quebrada, disse-lhe que fosse chamar seu marido. Não tenho marido. Ela respondeu. Jesus disse, bem disseste que não tens marido, porque tiveste cinco maridos, e o que agora tens não é teu marido. Nisto disseste a verdade. Ela percebeu que ele era um profeta. tentou mudar de assunto para a religião, perguntando onde era o lugar correto de adoração. Jesus disse-lhe que vinha a hora em que os verdadeiros adoradores adorariam o Pai em espírito e em verdade. Então, a mulher mencionou o Messias. Jesus olhou para ela e revelou claramente sua identidade. Eu o sou. Eu que falo contigo. Ela deixou seu cântaro e correu de volta à cidade. Disse a todos que viessem ver um homem que lhe havia dito tudo quanto ela fez e perguntou se aquele não seria o Messias. Por causa do testemunho dela, muitos samaritanos creram nele. Quando Jesus voltou a Caná, um oficial do rei veio até ele. Seu filho estava doente e perto da morte em Cafarnaum, que ficava a quilômetros de distância. O pai implorou que Jesus fosse curar seu filho. Jesus simplesmente disse: "Vai, o teu filho vive". O homem creu na palavra de Jesus e partiu. Enquanto ainda estava no caminho, seus servos vieram ao seu encontro com a notícia de que o menino estava vivo. O pai perguntou a que hora o menino havia melhorado. Disseram que no dia anterior, à 1 hora da tarde, a febre o havia deixado. O pai percebeu que aquela era exatamente a hora em que Jesus havia pronunciado as palavras. Ele e toda a sua casa creram. Isto mostrou que o poder de Jesus não é limitado pela distância. Algum tempo depois, Jesus subiu a Jerusalém. Havia um tanque chamado Betesdá, onde muitas pessoas com deficiências jaziam cegos, mancos e paralíticos. Um homem estava ali havia 38 anos. Jesus perguntou-lhe se ele queria ficar curado. O homem explicou que não tinha ninguém para colocá-lo no tanque quando a água era agitada. Jesus não o ajudou a entrar na água. Em vez disso, pronunciou um comando: "Levanta-te, toma o teu leito e anda." Imediatamente o homem ficou curado, pegou seu leito e andou. Mas isto aconteceu num sábado. Os líderes judeus viram o homem carregando seu leito e disseram-lhe que era contra a lei. Quando descobriram que Jesus o havia curado, começaram a persegui-lo. Jesus respondeu: "Meu pai trabalha até agora e eu trabalho também." Isto deixou os líderes ainda mais furiosos, pois ele estava chamando Deus de seu próprio pai, fazendo-se igual a Deus. Jesus atravessou para o outro lado do mar da Galileia. Uma grande multidão o seguia, porque viam os sinais que ele fazia nos enfermos. Estava perto da Páscoa judaica. Jesus viu a multidão e perguntou a Felipe onde poderiam comprar pão para todas aquelas pessoas. Felipe respondeu que 200 denários de pão seriam suficientes para que cada um recebesse um pedaço. Então, André falou, dizendo que ali estava um menino com cinco pães de cevada e dois peixinhos, mas perguntou o que era aquilo para tantos. Jesus mandou que as pessoas se sentassem na grama. Eram cerca de 5000 homens. Jesus tomou os pães, deu graças e os distribuiu. Fez o mesmo com os peixes. Todos comeram quanto quiseram. Quando estavam saciados, Jesus disse para recolherem os pedaços que sobraram, para que nada se perdesse. Encheram 12 cestos com as sobras. Naquela noite, os discípulos entraram num barco para atravessar o lago. Um vento forte começou a soprar e as águas ficaram agitadas. De repente, viram Jesus caminhando sobre as águas, aproximando-se do barco. Ficaram aterrorizados, mas ele lhes disse: "Sou eu, não tenhais medo." No dia seguinte, a multidão encontrou Jesus do outro lado. Queriam mais comida, mas Jesus os desafiou. Disse-lhes que não trabalhassem pela comida que perece, mas pela que permanece para a vida eterna. Então Jesus fez uma declaração ousada. Eu sou o pão da vida. Aquele que vem a mim jamais terá fome e quem crê em mim jamais terá sede. Muitos dos seus seguidores acharam o ensinamento difícil demais e voltaram atrás. Jesus perguntou aos 12 se eles também queriam retirar-se. Simão Pedro respondeu: "Senhor, para quem iremos? Tu tens palavras de vida eterna." Jesus foi a Jerusalém para a festa dos tabernáculos. Os líderes religiosos tentavam prendê-lo, mas sua hora ainda não havia chegado. Jesus levantou-se nos pátios do templo e ensinou, declarou: "Eu sou a luz do mundo. Quem me segue não andará nas trevas, mas terá a luz da vida." Os fariseus desafiaram seu testemunho. Seguiu-se um debate acalorado. Jesus disse-lhes que eles eram de baixo, mas ele era do alto. Disse-lhes que se permanecessem em sua palavra, conheceriam a verdade, e a verdade os libertaria. A discussão se intensificou até que os líderes religiosos reivindicaram Abraão como seu pai. Jesus disse-lhes que Abraão, pai deles, exultou por ver o seu dia. Viu-o e alegrou-se. Eles responderam que ele ainda não tinha 50 anos e perguntaram como poderia ter visto Abraão. Em verdade vos digo respondeu Jesus, antes que Abraão existisse, eu sou. Ao usar o título, Sou. Jesus estava reivindicando [limpando a garganta] o próprio nome de Deus. Eles pegaram pedras para matá-lo, mas ele se escondeu e saiu. Enquanto Jesus caminhava, viu um homem cego de nascença. Seus discípulos perguntaram: "Quem havia pecado? Aquele homem ou seus pais, para que nascesse cego? Nem ele pecou, nem seus pais", respondeu Jesus. Mas isto aconteceu para que se manifestassem nele as obras de Deus. Jesus cuspiu no chão, fez um pouco de lama com a saliva e a colocou nos olhos do homem. Disse-lhe que fosse lavar-se no tanque de Siloé. O homem foi, lavou-se e voltou enxergando. Os vizinhos ficaram admirados. Levaram o homem aos fariseus. Como Jesus havia feito a lama no sábado, os fariseus alegaram que ele era um pecador. Interrogaram o homem e seus pais. O homem respondeu que se ele era pecador, ele não sabia. Mas uma coisa ele sabia, era cego e agora via. O homem acabou concluindo que Jesus devia ser de Deus. Os fariseus ficaram furiosos e o expulsaram da sinagoga. Jesus ouviu isto e foi ao seu encontro. O homem creu em Jesus e o adorou. Jesus usou uma imagem da vida cotidiana para explicar seu amor. Descreveu um aprisco de ovelhas. O ladrão vem só para roubar, matar e destruir. Mas Jesus tem um propósito diferente. Eu vim, ele disse, para que tenham vida e a tenham em abundância. Então disse algo igualmente ousado. Eu sou a porta das ovelhas. Continuou: "Eu sou o bom pastor. O bom pastor dá a sua vida pelas ovelhas." Explicou que tinha outras ovelhas que não eram daquele aprisco, referindo-se aos gentios, e que precisava trazê-las também. Haveria um só rebanho e um só pastor. A história agora nos leva ao povoado de Betânia, a uma curta caminhada de Jerusalém. Ali vivia uma família muito próxima de Jesus. Duas irmãs, Maria e Marta, e seu irmão Lázaro. Não eram apenas conhecidos, eram seus queridos amigos. Mas uma crise atingiu aquele lar. Lázaro adoeceu gravemente. As irmãs fizeram o que qualquer um de nós faria num momento de desespero. Enviaram um mensageiro a Jesus. Nem mesmo pediram que ele viesse, apenas declararam o problema, confiando em seu amor. A mensagem foi curta e dilacer: "Senhor, aquele a quem amas está doente". Quando Jesus ouviu isto, sua resposta foi desconcertante. Não entrou em pânico, não correu para arrumar suas coisas. Em vez disso, fez uma declaração estranha. disse que aquela enfermidade não era para a morte, mas para a glória de Deus, para que o filho de Deus fosse glorificado por meio dela. Então está escrito algo que parece contradizer-se. Diz que Jesus amava Marta, sua irmã, e Lázaro. No entanto, quando soube que Lázaro estava doente, permaneceu mais dois dias onde estava. Imagine a confusão dos discípulos. Imagine a agonia das irmãs olhando para a estrada, esperando Jesus chegar apenas para verem o sol se pôr dia após dia sem ele. Por que ele esperaria? Em nossa compreensão humana, amor significa correr para ajudar. Mas Jesus operava em uma linha do tempo diferente. Não estava apenas planejando uma cura, estava planejando uma revelação que abalaria os próprios fundamentos da morte. Finalmente, depois de dois dias, Jesus disse aos seus discípulos que voltassem para a Judeia. Os discípulos ficaram aterrorizados. Lembraram a Jesus que os líderes religiosos de lá haviam acabado de tentar apedrejá-lo. Voltar era uma sentença de morte, mas Jesus estava determinado. Disse-lhes claramente que Lázaro estava morto e que por causa deles ele se alegrava de não ter estado lá para que cressem. Tomé, frequentemente lembrado por sua dúvida, mostrou aqui uma coragem incrível. Olhou para os outros discípulos e disse que fossem também para morrerem com ele. Quando Jesus finalmente chegou a Betânia, o pior havia acontecido. Lázaro já estava no túmulo havia quatro dias. Este detalhe é crucial. Na tradição judaica daquele tempo, havia a crença de que o espírito de uma pessoa poderia pairar perto do corpo por três dias. Mas ao quarto dia, a esperança havia desaparecido. A decomposição havia começado. A situação era completamente irreversível. Marta, sempre a mais ativa, ouviu que Jesus estava chegando e saiu ao seu encontro. Enquanto Maria ficou em casa sentada em luto. Marta disse a Jesus o que devia ter passado em sua mente vezes sem conta. Senhor, se tivesses estado aqui, meu irmão não teria morrido. Não era apenas uma declaração de fato, era um grito de dor. Mas mesmo em sua tristeza, ela se agarrava a um vislumbre de fé. Disse a Jesus que sabia que Deus lhe daria tudo o que ele pedisse. Jesus olhou para ela e prometeu que seu irmão ressuscitaria. Marta pensou que ele falava do futuro distante, da ressurreição final no fim dos tempos. Mas Jesus a interrompeu. Queria que ela compreendesse que a ressurreição não era apenas um evento no calendário, era uma pessoa de pé diante dela. Ele pronunciou a quinta e talvez mais poderosa declaração de Eu sou do Evangelho. Eu sou a ressurreição e a vida. Quem crê em mim, ainda que morra, viverá. E todo aquele que vive e crê em mim, jamais morrerá. Crês isto? Marta fez uma corajosa confissão de fé, chamando-o de Messias, e foi buscar sua irmã. Maria veio correndo e caiu aos pés de Jesus. chorou, repetindo as mesmas palavras que sua irmã havia dito quando Jesus a viu chorando e também os judeus que vieram com ela chorando, comoveu-se profundamente em espírito e perturbou-se. Frequentemente pensamos em Deus como distante ou sem emoções, mas aqui vemos o coração de Deus partindo-se por seus amigos. Ele perguntou onde haviam colocado Lázaro. Então encontramos o versículo mais curto de toda a Bíblia e um dos mais profundos. Jesus chorou. Ele sabia que estava prestes a ressuscitar Lázaro. Sabia da alegria que viria. Mesmo assim, parou para chorar. Entrou no sofrimento deles, sentiu o aguilhão da morte que tem assolado a humanidade desde o princípio. As pessoas que assistiam disseram: "Vede como ele o amava! Jesus, ainda gemendo em espírito, chegou ao túmulo. Era uma caverna com uma pedra colocada à entrada. Ele deu uma ordem que chocou a todos. Tirai a pedra, Marta, sempre prática." Protestou. advertiuo de que a esta altura já cheiraria mal, pois estava morto havia quatro dias. Estava tentando proteger a dignidade do irmão e os sentimentos de todos os presentes. Mas Jesus olhou para ela e relembrou-lhe sua promessa, perguntando se ele não havia dito que se ela cresse, veria a glória de Deus. Então tiraram a pedra. Um silêncio deve ter caído sobre a multidão. A boca escura e aberta da caverna estava amostra. Jesus olhou para o céu. Não orou por poder. Orou em benefício das pessoas que assistiam para que soubessem que o Pai o havia enviado. Então Jesus chamou em alta voz. Não sussurrou. Gritou para dentro das mandíbulas da morte. Lázaro vem para fora. Imagine a tensão daquele momento, o silêncio e então o movimento na escuridão. O morto saiu, vinha arrastando-se, com as mãos e os pés envoltos em faixas de linho e um pano em volta do rosto. Estava vivo. Jesus disse-lhes que o desatassem e o deixassem ir. Isto não foi um truque de mágica. foi o criador comandando sua criação. Ele inverteu o processo da morte. Este milagre era innegável. Muitos dos judeus que haviam vindo visitar Maria e viram o que Jesus fez, creram nele. No entanto, este grande ato de vida pôs em movimento as engrenagens da morte para Jesus. Algumas das testemunhas foram aos fariseus e contaram o que Jesus havia feito. O sumo sacerdote Caifás e o conselho reuniram-se com medo. Perceberam que se o deixassem continuar assim, todos creriam nele. E os romanos viriam e tomariam sua nação. Daquele dia em diante conspiraram para tirar-lhe a vida. A ressurreição de Lázaro é o ponto de virada na história. Mostra-nos que Jesus tem autoridade absoluta sobre a vida e a morte, mas também nos mostra o custo para devolver a vida a Lázaro. Jesus em breve teria que entregar a sua própria. O sol havia se posto e a escuridão se acumulava sobre Jerusalém. Era a noite anterior à Páscoa, a noite mais sagrada do ano judaico. Jesus sabia que sua hora havia chegado. Sabia que estava prestes a deixar este mundo e ir para o Pai. Mas antes de enfrentar a cruz, queria mostrar aos seus discípulos toda a extensão de seu amor. Reuniram-se em um cenáculo mobiliado para partilhar sua última refeição juntos. A atmosfera deveria ter sido solene, mas havia uma tensão subjacente. Os discípulos ainda estavam preocupados com o status e quem era o mais importante entre eles. Naquela cultura, o trabalho de lavar a poeira dos pés dos hóspedes pertencia ao servo mais humilde da casa. Mas não havia servo presente, e nenhum dos discípulos estava disposto a se rebaixar para fazer aquilo. Então Jesus fez o impensável. O criador do universo levantou-se da mesa, tirou a sua veste exterior e cingiu-se com uma toalha. Derramou água numa bacia e começou a lavar os pés dos discípulos, enxugando-os com a toalha. A sala deve ter caído em um silêncio atônito. O silêncio quebrou-se quando Jesus chegou a Simão Pedro. Pedro ficou horrorizado ao ver seu mestre ajoelhado diante dele e perguntou se ele realmente lavaria seus pés. Jesus respondeu que o que fazia naquele momento Pedro não compreendia, mas que entenderia depois. Pedro, em seu orgulho, recusou e disse que Jesus jamais lavaria seus pés. Jesus respondeu que se não o lavasse, ele não teria parte com ele. Pedro, percebendo que aquela lavagem simbolizava um relacionamento com Jesus, imediatamente foi para o outro extremo, pedindo a Jesus que lhe lavasse também as mãos e a cabeça. Mas Jesus disse-lhe que ele já estava limpo, apenas precisava ter os pés lavados. Quando Jesus terminou, vestiu-se novamente e voltou ao seu lugar. Olhou para eles e perguntou se compreendiam o que havia feito. Estava ensinando-lhes que em seu reino o líder é aquele que serve. Pois ele disse: "Se eu, Senhor e Mestre, vos lavei os pés, também vós deveis lavar os pés uns dos outros. Mas a intimidade da noite foi atravessada por uma realidade sombria. Jesus estava perturbado em espírito. Revelou que um dos homens que comiam com ele iria traí-lo. Mergulhou um pedaço de pão e o entregou a Judas Iscariotes. Assim que Judas tomou o pão, Satanás entrou nele. Jesus disse-lhe que o que estava para fazer fizesse depressa. Judas saiu para a noite. Com o traidor fora, Jesus podia agora falar livremente com sua verdadeira família. Ele lhes deu um legado, uma regra de vida que definiria a comunidade dos seus seguidores para sempre. Um novo mandamento vos dou. Ele disse que vos ameis uns aos outros, como eu vos amei, que também vós vos ameis uns aos outros. Nisto todos conhecerão que sois meus discípulos. Se tiverdes amor uns aos outros. Após a saída de Judas, o clima na sala mudou para o medo. Jesus disse-lhes claramente que estava indo embora e que eles não podiam segui-lo ainda. Pedro perguntou por não podia seguir, declarando que daria sua vida por Jesus. Mas Jesus previu que antes que o galo cantasse, Pedro o negaria três vezes. Vendo o pânico em seus olhos, Jesus pronunciou palavras de profundo conforto: "Não se turbe o vosso coração", ele disse, "credes em Deus, crede também em mim. Na casa de meu Pai há muitas moradas. Se assim não fora, eu vlo teria dito: Vou preparar-vos lugar." Ele prometeu que voltaria para levá-los a estarem com ele. Disse-lhes que conheciam o caminho para o lugar aonde ele ia. Mas Tomé, honesto e confuso, falou e disse que eles não sabiam para onde ele ia. Então, como poderiam saber o caminho? Tomé buscava um mapa, mas Jesus apontou para si mesmo. Pronunciou a sexta grande declaração de Eu sou. Eu sou o caminho, a verdade e a vida. Ninguém vem ao Pai senão por mim. Esta foi uma reivindicação de exclusividade e divindade. Jesus não é apenas um dos muitos caminhos para Deus. Ele é o único caminho. Felipe então pediu a Jesus que lhes mostrasse o Pai. Jesus respondeu com uma revelação impressionante de sua identidade. Quem me viu? Ele disse, "Viu [limpando a garganta] o Pai. Queria que compreendessem que Deus não é uma força distante e sem rosto. Se você quer saber como Deus é, olhe para Jesus." Jesus sabia que os discípulos sentiam-se abandonados. Ele estava partindo e eles se sentiam órfãos. Mas Jesus prometeu-lhes que sua partida era, na verdade, algo bom. Se ficasse, só poderia estar em um lugar de cada vez. Mas se fosse para o Pai, enviaria um auxiliador que poderia estar com eles em todos os lugares. Ele rogaria ao Pai, e o Pai lhes daria outro consolador, para que estivesse com eles para sempre o Espírito da verdade. Jesus explicou que o mundo não pode recebê-lo porque não o vê nem o conhece, mas os discípulos o conhecem, pois habita com eles e estará neles. Este foi um ensinamento revolucionário. Deus não habitaria mais em um templo de pedra. Habitaria dentro do coração do seu povo. Jesus explicou que o Espírito Santo lhes ensinaria todas as coisas e os faria lembrar de tudo o que ele havia dito. Também os deixou com um presente que o mundo nunca poderia dar. Deixo-vos a paz, ele disse, a minha paz vos dou. Não vou lá dou como o mundo a dá. Não se turbe o vosso coração, nem se atemorize. Mas Jesus também foi sincero quanto ao futuro. Advertiu-os de que o mundo os odiaria assim como o havia odiado. Disse-lhes que teriam tribulações. Comparou sua tristeza vindoura a uma mulher em trabalho de parto. A dor é intensa e real, mas é temporária e leva à nova vida. Estas coisas vos disse. Ele falou, para que em mim tenhais paz. No mundo passareis por aflições, mas tende bom ânimo. Eu venci o mundo. Enquanto se preparavam para deixar o cenáculo, Jesus deu-lhes uma última e poderosa metáfora para explicar como deveriam sobreviver e prosperar sem sua presença física. provavelmente passou pelos vinhedos que cresciam ao redor de Jerusalém, ou talvez tenha apontado para o fruto da videira sobre a mesa. Disse: "Eu sou a videira verdadeira e meu pai é o agricultor. Esta é a sétima e última declaração de Eu Sou". Explicou que um ramo não pode produzir fruto por si só, precisa permanecer ligado à videira. Da mesma forma, os discípulos não poderiam fazer nada de valor espiritual a menos que permanecessem ligados a ele. "Eu sou a videiras", ele disse. Vós sois os ramos. "Quem permanece em mim e eu nele, esse dá muito fruto. Sem mim nada podeis fazer". A palavra chave aqui é permanecer. Significa fazer dele a sua morada, manter-se ligado por meio da oração, da obediência e do amor. Se um ramo é quebrado, ele murcha e é lançado ao fogo. Mas se permanece ligado, a vida da videira flui através dele, produzindo uvas. Jesus definiu este fruto como amor. Repetiu seu mandamento de amarem-se uns aos outros, mas desta vez definiu o que aquele amor parecia. É sacrificial. Ninguém tem maior amor do que aquele que dá a sua vida pelos seus amigos. Disse-lhes que não os chamava mais de servos, pois um servo não conhece o que faz o seu senhor. Em vez disso, chamou-os de amigos, escolheu-os e os designou para ir e dar fruto, fruto que permanecesse. Após terminar seu ensino, Jesus não olhou para os discípulos, olhou para o céu. Está registrada esta longa e íntima conversa entre o Filho e o Pai. é frequentemente chamada de oração sacerdotal, porque Jesus se coloca na brecha pelo seu povo. Primeiro, Jesus orou por si mesmo. Sabia que a cruz o aguardava. Pai, ele disse, é chegada a hora. glorifica o teu filho para que o filho te glorifique. Não pediu para escapar da dor. Pediu que, por meio de sua morte, o verdadeiro caráter e amor de Deus fossem revelados ao mundo. Em seguida, orou pelos seus discípulos. Sabia que os deixava em um mundo hostil. Não pediu a Deus que os tirasse do mundo, mas que os protegesse do maligno. Orou para que fossem santificados ou separados pela verdade. Santifica-os na verdade. Ele orou. A tua palavra é a verdade. Finalmente, Jesus fez algo extraordinário. Olhou pelo corredor do tempo e orou por você. Não rogo somente por estes, ele disse, mas também por aqueles que crerão em mim por intermédio da palavra deles, a fim de que todos sejam um Pai, como tu estás em mim e eu em ti. Seu maior desejo para seus seguidores futuros era a unidade. Orou para que fôssemos levados à perfeita unidade, para que o mundo soubesse que Deus enviou Jesus e os ama. encerrou sua oração com uma declaração de amor e uma promessa de sua presença, dizendo que havia dado a conhecer o nome do Pai e ainda o faria conhecer, a fim de que o amor com que o Pai o amou estivesse neles e ele neles estivesse. Com esta oração concluída, Jesus havia terminado seu ministério de ensino. Agora estava pronto para enfrentar o sacrifício. atravessou o Vale do Cedrom com seus discípulos e entrou em um olival, onde a traição o aguardava. Judas conhecia o lugar. Chegou com um destacamento de soldados carregando tochas, lanternas e armas. Jesus, sabendo de tudo o que estava para acontecer com ele, saiu e perguntou a quem buscavam. A Jesus de Nazaré, responderam: "Sou eu", disse Jesus. Quando Jesus disse: "Sou eu", recuaram e caíram por terra. Mesmo em sua prisão, Jesus tinha o controle. Simão Pedro, que tinha uma espada, sacou-a e feriu o servo do sumo sacerdote, cortando-lhe a orelha direita. O nome do servo era Malco. Mas Jesus disse a Pedro que guardasse a espada, perguntando se ele não havia de beber o cálice que o pai lhe dera. Permitiu então que o amarrassem. Levaram no primeiro a Anás, sogro de Caifás, que era o sumo sacerdote naquele ano. Pedro o seguiu de longe, junto com outro discípulo. Estava frio, e Pedro ficou junto aos servos e oficiais ao redor de uma fogueira para se aquecer. Enquanto estava ali, perguntaram-lhe três vezes se era um dos discípulos de Jesus. Três vezes Pedro negou, dizendo que não era. Naquele momento, um galo cantou. Jesus foi então levado ao governador romano Pôncio Pilatos. Encarando o prisioneiro, ele perguntou se Jesus era o rei dos judeus. Jesus disse que o seu reino não era deste mundo. Se o seu reino fosse deste mundo, seus servos teriam lutado para que ele não fosse entregue aos judeus. Mas na verdade o seu reino não era dali. Que é a verdade, perguntou [limpando a garganta] o governador. Ele não encontrou nenhum motivo para acusar Jesus. Ofereceu-se para libertá-lo conforme o costume, mas a multidão gritou que ele não, mas Barrabá. Barrabá era um revolucionário. Pilatos então mandou açoitar Jesus. Os soldados teceram uma coroa de espinhos e a colocaram em sua cabeça. Vestiram-no com um manto de púrpura e zombaram dele, dizendo: "Salve, rei dos judeus!" Embora tentasse libertá-lo, os líderes gritaram que o crucificasse. Por fim, o governante romano o entregou para ser crucificado. Jesus carregou sua própria cruz para um lugar chamado o lugar da caveira, que em aramaico se chama Glgota. Ali o crucificaram com outros dois, um de cada lado. Foi preparada uma inscrição e fixada na cruz. Estava escrita em hebraico, latim e grego, e dizia Jesus de Nazaré, o rei dos judeus. Junto à cruz de Jesus estava sua mãe. Quando Jesus a viu ali e ao discípulo amado de pé ao lado dela, disse a ela: "Mulher, eis aí o teu filho". E ao discípulo: "Eis aí a tua mãe". Mais tarde, sabendo que tudo já estava consumado, Jesus disse: "Tenho sede". Havia ali um vaso cheio de vinagre. Embeberam uma esponja em vinagre e a levaram à sua boca. Quando recebeu o vinagre, Jesus disse: "Está consumado". Inclinou a cabeça e entregou o espírito. Era o dia da preparação para o sábado. Os soldados vieram quebrar as pernas dos homens para apressar sua morte. Mas quando chegaram a Jesus, viram que já estava morto, e por isso não lhe quebraram os ossos. Em vez disso, um dos soldados perfurou o lado de Jesus com uma lança, e imediatamente saiu sangue e água. Tudo isso aconteceu para que se cumprissem as escrituras, que diziam que nenhum osso dele seria quebrado e ainda que olhariam para aquele a quem traspassaram. José de Arimateia, que era discípulo de Jesus em segredo por temor dos judeus, pediu a Pilatos que pudesse levar o corpo. Veio também Nicodemos, aquele que de início procurara Jesus à noite, trazendo uma mistura de mirra e aloés. Tomaram o corpo de Jesus e o envolveram em faixas de linho, com as especiarias, conforme o costume judaico de sepultamento. Colocaram-no em um túmulo novo, em um jardim próximo, onde ainda ninguém havia sido sepultado. Bem cedo, no primeiro dia da semana, ainda escuro, Maria Madalena foi ao túmulo, viu que a pedra havia sido removida da entrada. Correu para contar a Simão Pedro e ao outro discípulo, dizendo que haviam tirado o Senhor do túmulo e que não sabiam onde o haviam posto. Pedro e o outro discípulo correram ao túmulo. O outro discípulo chegou primeiro, mas não entrou. Pedro chegou e entrou no túmulo. Viu as faixas de linho ali, bem como o pano que havia sido enrolado em volta da cabeça de Jesus. O pano ainda estava em seu lugar, separado das faixas. O outro discípulo também entrou, viu e creu. Voltaram para onde estavam hospedados. Mas Maria ficou do lado de fora do túmulo chorando. Virou-se e viu Jesus de pé ali, mas não percebeu que era ele. Mulher, ele disse, por que choras? A quem procuras? Pensando que era o jardineiro, ela disse que se ele havia levado o corpo, que lhe dissesse onde o havia posto e ela o tomaria. Jesus disse-lhe: "Maria". Ela voltou-se para ele e exclamou em aramaico: "Rabone, que significa mestre". Maria Madalena foi aos discípulos com a notícia de que havia visto o Senhor.
